3     Recursive Construction
In this section we present a recursive construction using holey SOLS and
use it to show the existence of CMDRR(n, k) for all allowed values of n
and k, apart from 4 exceptions and 31 possible exceptions. We show that
a fully resolvable CMDRR(2n, 0) exists for all n ≥ 5 and a fully resolvable
CMDRR(3n, n) exists for all n ≥ 5 and n odd.
    For completeness we include the definition of a holey SOLS (see [10]).

Definition 3.1 A holey SOLS (or frame SOLS) is a self-orthogonal latin
square of order n with ni missing sub-SOLS           P(holes) of order hi (1 ≤ i ≤ k),
which are disjoint and spanning (that is 1≤i≤k ni hi = n). It is denoted
by HSOLS(hn1 1 . . . hnk k ) where hn1 1 . . . hnk k is the type of the HSOLS.

   Suppose an HSOLS exists and CMDRR exist for each hole size. Then
we can fill in the holes with the CMDRRs to get a new CMDRR. The
details follow. For convenience we will assume that a CMDRR(1, 1) exists
with spouse pair M1F1 and no games.

Theorem 3.2 Suppose an HSOLS(n) of type hn1 1 . . . hnk k exists and for
each hi there exist CMDRR(hi , mP             CMDRR(hi , mini ). Then there
                                i1 ), . . . , P
exists a CMDRR(n, s) where s = 1≤i≤k 1≤j≤ni mij .

Proof: By possibly relabeling players we can assume that the HSOLS is
block diagonal. By construction, spouse pairs will always have the form
MiFi.
    Use the standard SOLS representation for a SAMDRR to identify games
for all entries of the HSOLS that are not in a hole. Thus every entry (i, j)
not in a hole will contribute the game MiF(i, j) v MjF(j, i). By definition
of an HSOLS these games satisfy the conditions that every pair of opposite

                                                                          11


sex players have partnered and opposed at most once, every pair of same
sex players have opposed at most once, and no spouses have played in a
game together.
    Each missing sub-SOLS (hole) corresponds to a set S of consecutive in-
tegers which are the indices and also the missing entries. Use a translation
of an appropriate sized CMDRR and identify games using the represen-
tation introduced for CMDRRs. Assume the translation is by t. Then a
non-empty entry (i, j) of the CMDRR will contribute the game Mi′ Mj ′
v Fx′ Fy ′ where the (i, j) entry of the CMDRR is (x, y) and each primed
symbol is the corresponding unprimed symbol plus t. By definition of an
HSOLS, the players in a hole will not be involved in any other common
games.
    Taking all the games identified by the two different processes described
above will produce the required CMDRR. As every pair of players is either
in a hole or not in any hole, the conditions for a CMDRR are met.

Example 3.3 A CMDRR(11, 7) can be constructed from the
HSOLS(16 31 21 ) given below (see [10, 16]). Use the CMDRR(3, 1) of Exam-
ple 1.3 to fill the hole of size three and the strict MMDRR(2) of Example 2.1
to fill the hole of size two. The spouse pairs are M1F1, . . . ,M7F7. The re-
peat oppositions are M8M9, F8F9, M10M11, and F10F11.
   .     7     5     2 10 9           4 11 3 6 8
  6      .     8     7    3 10 1           5 11 4 9
  9      4     .    10 8        1 11 2           6 5 7
  5 11 9             .    7     2     6 10 1 8 3
  7      6 11 3           .     8     2    4 10 9 1
  11 8         4     9    1      .   10 3        5 7 2
  3 10 6             1 11 5           .    .     .    2 4
  4      1 10 6           2 11        .    .     .    3 5
  10 5         2 11 4           3     .    .     .    1 6
  8      9     7     5    6     4     3    1     2    . .
  2      3     1     8    9     7     5    6     4    . .

   In order to create CMDRR using Theorem 3.2 we need a supply of
HSOLS. The following theorems (see [10, 12, 15]) give just the supply we
need.

Theorem 3.4 For n ≥ 4 and a ≥ 2, an HSOLS(an b1 ) exists if 0 ≤ b ≤
a(n−1)/2 with possible exceptions for n ∈ {6, 14, 18, 22} and b = a(n−1)/2.

Definition 3.5 An incomplete SOLS is a self-orthogonal latin square of
order n missing a sub-SOLS of order k, denoted by ISOLS(n, k). An
ISOLS(n, k) is equivalent to an HSOLS(1n−k k 1 ). (see [10])

                                                                                       12


Theorem 3.6 There exists an ISOLS(n, k) for all values of n and k sat-
isfying n ≥ 3k + 1, except for (n, k) = (6, 1), (8, 2) and possibly excepting
n = 3k + 2 and k ∈ {6, 8, 10}.

    We can now show that CMDRR(n, k) exist for all but a finite number
of possible exceptions.

Theorem 3.7 There exists a CMDRR(n, k) for each n ≥ 32, k ≤ n, and
n − k even.

Proof: By Theorem 3.4 an HSOLS(an b1 ) exists for a = 8, n ≥ 4, and
0 ≤ b ≤ 7. Each hole of size 8 can be filled with any one of the CMDRR(8, 0)
(Example 2.11), CMDRR(8, 2) (Example 2.12), CMDRR(8, 4)
(Example 2.13), CMDRR(8, 6) (Example 2.14), or a SAMDRR(8). For
2 ≤ b ≤ 7, the hole of size b can be filled with one of the CMDRR(b, i)
given in Examples 2.1– 2.10 or the SAMDRR(b) for b 6= 2, 3, 6. By direct
observation and using Theorem 3.4 we have the stated CMDRRs, except
for CMDRR(n, n) when n ≥ 32 and n ≡ 2, 3, or 6 (mod 8). But we know
that SAMDRR(n) exist for these sizes.
   Finally we can use Theorems 3.4 and 3.6 to handle special cases for
n < 32 and give our main result.

Theorem 3.8 There exists a CMDRR(n, k) for each n ≥ 2, k ≤ n, and
n−k even, except for (n, k) = (2, 2), (3, 3), (4, 2), (6, 6) and possibly except-
ing the following 31 values: (n, k) = (5, 3), (6, 2), (12, 2), (12, 6), (12, 8),
(13, 3), (13, 7), (14, 2), (14, 6), (15, 3), (15, 7), (15, 9), (16, 2), (16, 10), (17, 3),
(17, 7), (17, 11), (18, 2), (18, 10), (19, 3), (19, 11), (20, 2), (20, 14), (21, 3),
(21, 11), (22, 2), (22, 14), (23, 15), (24, 2), (24, 14), (25, 15).

Proof: By Theorem 3.7, a CMDRR(n, k) exists for n ≥ 32. The table
below shows a construction that can be used for each value of n < 32 and
compatible k, except for the values listed above.

    Resolvability of a CMDRR is more difficult to ensure. By filling holes
in an HSOLSSOM we can construct resolvable CMDRR. For completeness
we include the definition of a holey SOLSSOM (see [10]).
Definition 3.9 A holey SOLSSOM (or frame SOLSSOM) is a holey self-
orthogonal latin square S of order n and type hn1 1 . . . hnk k , together with
a symmetric partitioned latin square M of order n and type hn1 1 . . . hnk k ,
satisfying the property that when superimposed, the ordered pairs are exactly
those pairs of symbols that are from different holes. A holey SOLSSOM with
this structure is denoted by HSOLSSOM(hn1 1 . . . hnk k ), where hn1 1 . . . hnk k is
the type of the HSOLSSOM.
   Using the next Theorem [10, 11] we can construct resolvable CMDRR.
Theorem 3.10 An HSOLSSOM(2n ) exists for all n ≥ 5 and an
HSOLSSOM(3n) exists for all odd values of n with n ≥ 5.

FILE DORMANT-SOL-FORMAL-PLAN.md
# Dormant Sol formal plan — no dispatch or execution
Target MathDB326555 only, full source Definition1.2 nonexistence of CMDRR(5,3). Theorem should say no schedule exists for five men, five women, and exactly three disjoint spouse pairs. Arbitrary spouse labeling must be transported to canonical pairs1,2,3; do not assume spouses originally share index.

Define games as unordered pairs of mixed partnerships with distinct male/female endpoints. Define schedule with exact multiplicity axioms: spouses never share a game, each other mixed pair partners once and opposes once, each spouse player opposes every same-sex player once, singles oppose their unique other single twice and all spouses once. No rounds/resolvability axiom. Finite game identities must respect duplicate representations; partnership uniqueness supplies a bijection with22cells modulo opposite teams.

Prove canonical-cell equivalence, not just a graph proxy: X is Fin5×Fin5 minus three spouse cells. Opposite-team permutation T is a fixed-point-free involution on X. Derived row U and column C are genuine permutations from mixed partnership/opposition uniqueness; prove C∘T=U. Derive same-sex counts using row/column restricted cells (one cell per game at a chosen player), not an unjustified division by2.

Prove spouse-row4cycle lemma without finite enumeration: no fixed points, only possible repeated female pair45, so two2cycles are impossible. Prove complementary-edge occurrence identity on a4cycle. Define game counts A,B,z and double-count to obtain A+B=1+z. If a single row has2cycle45, its remainder is3cycle; derive A=0 and B=2-z, contradiction2z=1 over natural numbers. Symmetric column argument. Then obtain three4cycles and two5cycles on each side, sign U=sign C=-1. Prove T has eleven2cycles from card X=22 and no fixed cells; sign T=-1 contradicts multiplicativity from C∘T=U. All these are proof obligations, not implemented claims.

Use ordinary finite permutations/cardinality/sign lemmas with structural proofs; no search through schedules, native_decide or external certificate. Pin Lean/mathlib only in a later explicitly authorized formal lane. Required audits: exact literal/source fidelity including k=spouse pairs, independent adversarial proof/significance review, all dependencies and #print axioms restricted to propext/Classical.choice/Quot.sound, fresh extracted strict rebuild, full paper, root acceptance. No sorry/admit/added axioms/unsafe. Do not dispatch from this lane. Current formal and acceptance credit zero.

      "title": "Necessary conditions for linear promotion eigenvalues of non-down-forest posets",
      "statement": "Let $P$ be a poset of size $n$ that is not a down forest, and let $M$ be its promotion transition matrix. Write the parameters as $x_1,\\dots,x_n$. **Necessary-conditions conjecture.** If all eigenvalues of $M$ are linear in $x_1,\\dots,x_n$, then: (1) every coefficient of every parameter in the eigenvalues is either $1$ or $-1$; (2) every element of $P$ has at most two successors; and (3) the only parameters whose coefficients in the eigenvalues are $-1$ are those corresponding either to elements with two successors or to elements having a successor with two successors. The paper presents these conditions as necessary but not sufficient, based on data for non-down-forest posets of sizes up to $7$; a complete classification of posets with linear eigenvalues remains open.",

      "statement": "Let $a<b$ be coprime. For an $(a,b)$-core partition $\\lambda$, let $\\ell(\\lambda)$ be its number of nonzero rows. Let $s\\ell(\\lambda)$ be its skew length, and define its co-skew length by\n\n$$\ns\\ell'(\\lambda)=\\frac{(a-1)(b-1)}{2}-s\\ell(\\lambda).\n$$\n\n**Bivariate symmetry conjecture.**\n\n$$\n\\sum_{\\lambda}q^{\\ell(\\lambda)}t^{s\\ell'(\\lambda)}=\\sum_{\\lambda}t^{\\ell(\\lambda)}q^{s\\ell'(\\lambda)},\n$$\n\nwhere both sums are over $(a,b)$-cores $\\lambda$. This is presented as a shadow of the broader $q,t$-Catalan theory and is expected to be difficult; no resolution is supplied in the source.",

      "statement": "Let $T_n$ be the full transformation semigroup on an $n$-element set. For a transformation $a\\in T_n$, call it **non-uniform** if its kernel classes are not all of the same size. A permutation group $G$ is **primitive** if it preserves no nontrivial partition of the underlying set, and it synchronizes $a$ when the semigroup generated by $G$ and $a$ contains a map of rank $1$.\n\n**Synchronization conjecture.** Every primitive permutation group synchronizes every non-uniform map.\n\nThis is presented as the biggest open problem in the area.       "statement": "Let $(G,H)$ be a pair of graphs with $H\\subset G$, let $\\mu$ be an integer, and let $\\Delta=\\Delta(H)\\geq 1$. A $\\mu(G,H)$-labeling is a $(G,H)$-labeling using labels from $\\{0,\\ldots,\\mu\\}$ with the additional condition that if $xy\\in E(H)$, then $|f(x)-f(y)|\\neq\\mu$. **Circular labeling conjecture.** Suppose that $\\Delta(G)\\leq\\Delta^2$ and $\\mu\\geq\\Delta^2+1$. Then a $\\mu(G,H)$-labeling exists whenever\n\n$$\n|V(G)|\\leq(\\mu-\\Delta)\\left(\\left\\lfloor\\frac{\\mu-2}{2\\Delta}\\right\\rfloor+1\\right)-2.\n$$\n\nThis is proposed as an extension of the paper's results to circular distance-two labelings; the source gives no resolution evidence.",

      "title": "The induced-subdivision dichotomy conjecture for digraphs",
      "statement": "Let $D$ be a digraph. For a digraph $G$, let $i_D$ denote the problem of deciding whether $G$ contains an induced subdivision of $D$. A **spider** is a tree obtained from disjoint directed paths by identifying one end of each path into a vertex, and $C_2$ is the digraph on two vertices with both possible arcs.\n\n**Induced-subdivision dichotomy conjecture.** $i_D$ is NP-complete unless $D$ is the disjoint union of spiders and at most one $2$-cycle.\n\nThis conjecture proposes the complexity dichotomy for induced-subdivision detection in digraphs; the preceding results establish the analogous statement for oriented graphs, while the general digraph case remains open.",

      "statement": "An **oriented matroid** is a combinatorial abstraction of the dependence relations among a set of real vectors together with their signs; a **positively oriented matroid** is an oriented matroid for which all bases have a positive orientation. A matroid is **realizable** if it can be represented by a set of vectors in a real vector space.\n\n**da Silva's conjecture.** Every positively oriented matroid is realizable.\n\nThe paper proves this conjecture more strongly by showing that every positively oriented matroid is a positroid, and hence is realizable over $\\mathbb{Q}$.",

      "statement": "Let $n$ be an even positive integer, and let $k$ be a positive integer. The $n$-dimensional hypercube $\\mathcal{Q}_n$ has vertex set $\\{0,1\\}^n$ and edges joining vertices at $\\ell_1$-distance one. A **path of length $k$** is a sequence of $k+1$ distinct vertices with consecutive vertices joined by an edge. **Even-dimensional path-decomposition conjecture.** If\n\n$$\nk\\mid n2^{n-1}\\quad\\text{and}\\quad k<2^n,\n$$\n\nthen $\\mathcal{Q}_n$ can be decomposed into paths of length $k$. This extends the path-decomposition question to the even-dimensional case, where the degree-parity obstruction forcing $k\\leq n$ in odd dimensions is absent. The source presents this as a conjecture without resolving it.",

      "title": "Ruzsa–Matolcsi conjecture on triangulations of planar sum sets",
      "statement": "Let $A$ and $B$ be finite non-collinear point sets in $\\mathbb{R}^2$. For a finite non-collinear set $S$, let $b_S$ and $i_S$ denote the numbers of points of $S$ on the boundary and in the interior of its convex hull, respectively. Since the number of triangles in a triangulation of the convex hull is $\\operatorname{tr}(S)=b_S+2i_S-2$, **Ruzsa–Matolcsi's conjecture.**\n\n$$\n\\sqrt{2i_{A+B}+b_{A+B}-2}\\geq \\sqrt{2i_A+b_A-2}+\\sqrt{2i_B+b_B-2}.\n$$\n\nEquivalently, $\\operatorname{tr}(A+B)^{\\frac12}\\geq \\operatorname{tr}(A)^{\\frac12}+\\operatorname{tr}(B)^{\\frac12}$. This is a planar discrete analogue of the Brunn–Minkowski inequality; the paper verifies it in special cases, while the general conjecture remains open.",
